(b) Case Study: A young project manager is leading a software development team for the first time. Team
members expect support, guidance, and clarity in task priorities.
Analyze how the manager can apply Servant Leadership principles in day-to-day practices to support the
team.
Synoptic Answer:
A young project manager leading a software development team for the first time can apply Servant
Leadership principles in day-to-day practices by focusing on serving the team’s needs first, providing
support, guidance, and clarity in task priorities to empower and enable the team to succeed. Key Practices
of Servant Leadership for the Manager: -
Active Listening: The manager should attentively listen to team members’ concerns, ideas, and feedback
without interruption.
Empathy: Understanding the perspectives and emotions of team members promotes a supportive
environment.
Clear Communication of Priorities: Servant leadership involves providing clarity in task priorities by
explaining the “why” behind project goals, enabling team members to align their efforts meaningfully.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Removing Obstacles: The manager should proactively identify and eliminate barriers that prevent the
team from achieving their goals, such as resource constraints or unclear processes.
Empowerment and Trust: Encouraging self-organization and decision-making within the team promotes
ownership and motivation. The manager acts as a coach and mentor rather than an authoritarian figure.
Personal and Professional Development: Supporting team members’ growth through mentoring, training
opportunities, and recognizing their achievements helps build a stronger, more capable team.
Collaboration and Community: Building a sense of belonging and teamwork strengthens relationships
and drives collective success.
Balancing Service and Leadership: While serving the team, the manager must also guide, make decisions,
and hold the team accountable for outcomes, maintaining focus on project objectives.
By integrating these servant leadership behaviors, the young project manager creates an environment
where the team feels supported, prioritized, and motivated to excel, ultimately driving successful project
delivery and team satisfaction. This approach not only addresses immediate needs for support and clarity
but cultivates a culture of trust and continuous improvement.
4.   Case - When India’s Chandrayaan-2 mission lost contact with its lander moments before touchdown,
silence filled the ISRO control room. Years of work, sleepless nights, and national hopes seemed to
collapse in a single instant. Scientists sat frozen, their faces lit by the dim glow of monitors. In that heavy
silence, ISRO Chairman Dr. K. Sivan rose from his seat. He did not search for someone to blame. Instead,
he faced his team and said softly, “We did everything we could. This is not a failure—it is a step forward.”
His voice carried calm assurance, cutting through the tension in the room. He moved from desk to desk,
speaking personally to the engineers and young scientists who had worked under him, telling them how
proud he was of their dedication. Many were on the verge of tears; he reminded them that no mission is
wasted when it teaches something valuable. The next morning, as the world watched, Dr. Sivan took full
responsibility before the media, calling the outcome “a learning experience” and emphasizing the team’s
achievements in bringing the mission so close to success. He avoided technical blame or excuses, choosing
instead to focus on the team’s spirit and resilience.
Later, when Prime Minister Modi arrived to encourage the scientists, Dr. Sivan, overwhelmed with emotion, broke
down. It was a rare, human moment for a scientist known for his composure. Yet that vulnerability only
strengthened his team’s respect for him. They saw a leader who stood with them in failure, protected them in public,
and guided them back to confidence. In the months that followed, his words became the team’s motivation as they
began preparing for the next mission, proof that leadership is not about command, but about compassion and
courage in moments of loss. [CO-1, CO-2; Bloom’s Taxonomy, L4 (Analyze), L5 (Evaluate)]
(a) Analyze the servant leadership style demonstrated by Dr. K. Sivan in this case through key servant
leader behaviors, including emotional healing, putting followers first, commitment to their growth,
empathy, and accountability. (10 Marks)
(b) Analyze the value-driven leadership style demonstrated by Dr. K. Sivan in this case through core
values, integrity, Empathy, Accountability, Humility and resilience. (10 Marks)
Synoptic Answer:
(a)
Emotional Healing: Immediately after the lander lost contact, Dr. Sivan focused on alleviating the team’s
distress. By moving from desk to desk, speaking personally to each engineer, and acknowledging their
efforts, he helped them process their disappointment and regain composure. His reassurance that “no
mission is wasted when it teaches something valuable” provided emotional support that mitigated feelings
of failure and despair.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Putting Followers First: Throughout the crisis, Dr. Sivan prioritized the well-being of his team over his
own public image or personal feelings. He shielded them from blame, publicly emphasizing their
achievements and dedication, and highlighted their collective contribution before taking personal
responsibility. This behavior demonstrated that the team’s morale and recognition were more important
than his own status.
Commitment to Growth: Dr. Sivan reframed the mission’s outcome as a learning experience, signaling
that mistakes are opportunities for improvement rather than reasons for reprimand. By encouraging
reflection and continued effort, he fostered a culture where scientists could grow professionally and
develop resilience, ensuring the organization benefited from both success and failure.
Empathy: His actions revealed deep empathy toward the team. Recognizing their emotional investment,
he validated their feelings, connected personally with those on the verge of tears, and openly displayed
his own vulnerability. This authenticity strengthened trust and reinforced the human dimension of
leadership.
Accountability: Dr. Sivan took full responsibility for the mission’s outcome in front of the media,
demonstrating ethical accountability. By owning the failure publicly while crediting the team’s efforts,
he modeled responsibility without scapegoating, reinforcing a culture of integrity and transparency.
(b) When the Chandrayaan-2 mission’s lander lost contact moments before touchdown, disappointment
swept through the ISRO control room. In that tense moment, Dr. K. Sivan did not retreat into technical
explanations or self-defense. Instead, he anchored his response in the core values that define ISRO’s
ethos; integrity, accountability, humility, empathy, and collective responsibility. He immediately took
responsibility for the outcome, emphasizing that the team had performed with utmost dedication. His
statement, “We did everything we could”, reflected integrity and transparency, showing the nation that
honest effort matters as much as results. Rather than assigning blame, he publicly acknowledged the
contribution of every scientist, from the senior engineers to the youngest interns. This act displayed
respect and equality, values deeply embedded in his leadership philosophy. Later, when he consoled team
members individually and even broke down emotionally, it revealed authenticity and compassion, values
that humanized leadership in a highly technical institution. His vulnerability did not weaken his authority;
it deepened trust, reinforcing the value of empathy in leadership.
In the following weeks, Dr. Sivan encouraged open discussions to analyze what went wrong, fostering a
culture of learning, honesty, and perseverance rather than fear or silence. By doing so, he embodied
accountability as a shared value, not a burden to be avoided.

(a) In a multicultural workplace, you notice cultural differences causing misunderstandings among
colleagues. How would you apply interpersonal skills such as active listening, cultural sensitivity, and
teamwork to create a collaborative and inclusive environment? (10 Marks)
Synoptic Answer:
I would start by actively listening to understand colleagues’ viewpoints and cultural contexts.
Demonstrating cultural sensitivity, I would avoid assumptions or judgments, recognizing that diverse
perspectives enrich problem-solving. Encouraging open dialogue, I would create opportunities for team
members to share ideas and express concerns comfortably. Promoting teamwork through inclusive
practices, such as rotating leadership roles or collaborative brainstorming sessions—ensures everyone
feels valued. Providing feedback respectfully and acknowledging contributions fosters mutual trust. By
applying empathy, patience, and awareness, I can reduce misunderstandings, enhance communication,
and build a cohesive, inclusive workplace where diversity strengthens collaboration and innovation.
(b) SparkFizz Beverages is a mid-sized soft drink company known for its affordable fruit-based sodas.
Over the last five years, the market has become increasingly competitive with the rise of health-conscious
consumers and eco-friendly startups. Sales of SparkFizz’s existing products have begun to decline,
especially among urban youth who prefer low-sugar or natural options. The company’s Managing Director
has set up a cross-functional innovation team led by Aarav, the Product Development Head, to create a
new beverage concept that can reposition SparkFizz as a youthful, health-oriented brand. Aarav wants the
team to use mind mapping as the starting tool for brainstorming and idea generation. He believes it can
help the group visually organize their thoughts, connect diverse insights, and identify unique opportunities
from different perspectives such as ingredients, packaging, sustainability, and marketing. As Aarav and
his team begin the product development process, design and justify how a mind map can be applied to
generate, connect, and refine innovative ideas for SparkFizz’s new health-oriented beverage.
Explain what key branches or categories you would include in the mind map and how this approach can
enhance creativity, collaboration, and clarity in decision-making. (10 marks)
Synoptic Answer:
A mind map can help SparkFizz’s team organize and expand their ideas visually while developing a new
health-oriented beverage. By starting with the main idea — “New Healthy Drink for SparkFizz”, the team
can branch out into key areas such as ingredients, target market, packaging, and marketing.
Under ingredients, they can explore natural options like fruit juices, herbs, or low-sugar alternatives. The
target audience branch can focus on health-conscious youth, working professionals, and fitness
enthusiasts. For packaging, the team might discuss eco-friendly bottles, recyclable materials, and
attractive label designs. In the marketing branch, they can add ideas like social media promotions, tie-ups
with gyms, or influencer campaigns. Other branches may include pricing, distribution channels, and future
product extensions like smoothies or hydration drinks.
Using a mind map allows the team to see connections between ideas, such as how natural ingredients link
with sustainability or how packaging supports brand image. It helps everyone contribute creatively, stay
organized, and build a complete product concept step by step.
In short, mind mapping encourages clear thinking, teamwork, and creativity, turning a simple idea into
an innovative, market-ready product.
6.   Rahul is a team leader in a software development company. Recently, one of his team members,
Anjali, has been underperforming and missing deadlines. Rahul notices that she seems stressed,
withdrawn, and less communicative during meetings. When he tries to discuss the issue, Anjali hesitates
to open up, fearing judgment or criticism. At the same time, other team members have started expressing
frustration due to delays in project deliverables. Rahul realizes that understanding Anjali’s perspective is
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
crucial to resolving the situation effectively. He decides to practice empathy—actively listening to her
concerns, acknowledging her feelings, and offering support while balancing team productivity. Rahul also
reflects on how fostering a supportive and understanding environment can improve team morale, reduce
stress, and enhance overall performance.
(a) Identify the key areas where Rahul can demonstrate empathy. How can active listening, understanding,
and emotional support help him address Anjali’s challenges while maintaining team effectiveness? (10
Marks)
(b) Suggest practical steps Rahul can take to create an empathetic work environment that encourages open
communication, reduces stress, and motivates employees to perform at their best. (10 Marks)
Synoptic Answer:
(a) Identify the key areas where Rahul can demonstrate empathy. How can active listening, understanding,
and emotional support help him address Anjali’s challenges while maintaining team effectiveness?
Rahul can demonstrate empathy in several key areas: actively listening to Anjali’s concerns without
interrupting, acknowledging her feelings to validate her stress and frustrations, and offering emotional
support by providing guidance and encouragement. By creating a safe, non-judgmental space, he helps
Anjali feel understood and comfortable sharing the reasons behind her underperformance. Active listening
allows Rahul to identify specific obstacles—whether workload, personal issues, or skill gaps—that may
be affecting her productivity. Understanding her perspective enables him to tailor support, such as
adjusting deadlines or offering assistance, without compromising overall project timelines. Emotional
support fosters trust and confidence, motivating Anjali to re-engage with her tasks. By combining empathy
with constructive solutions, Rahul can maintain team effectiveness, reduce friction caused by delays, and
reinforce a collaborative environment that balances individual well-being with collective performance.
(b) Suggest practical steps Rahul can take to create an empathetic work environment that encourages
open communication, reduces stress, and motivates employees to perform at their best.
Rahul can implement several practical measures to cultivate an empathetic work culture. First, he should
schedule regular one-on-one check-ins with team members to understand their challenges and offer
support. Second, he can encourage open communication, ensuring employees feel safe sharing ideas,
concerns, or feedback without fear of judgment. Third, he should recognize efforts and achievements,
boosting morale and motivation. Providing flexible workload management, where responsibilities are
balanced according to capacity, helps reduce stress. Additionally, promoting peer support and
collaboration allows team members to assist each other, fostering trust. Rahul can also introduce stress-
relief initiatives, like short breaks, team-building activities, or wellness programs, to maintain overall
well-being. By consistently practicing these strategies, Rahul can build a supportive, empathetic
environment that improves employee engagement, enhances productivity, and strengthens team cohesion.
